Returns texts, depths, doc_ids.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    texts, depths, doc_ids = [], [], []\\n    for d in range(max_depth + 1):\\n        path = data_dir / f\\\"depth_{d}.jsonl\\\"\\n        if not path.exists():\\n            log.warning(f\\\"Missing {path}, skipping depth {d}\\\")\\n            continue\\n        count = 0\\n        with open(path) as f:\\n            for line in f:\\n                if num_samples is not None and count >= num_samples:\\n                    break\\n                rec = json.loads(line)\\n                texts.append(rec[\\\"text\\\"])\\n                depths.append(d)\\n                doc_ids.append(rec[\\\"doc_id\\\"])\\n                count += 1\\n        log.info(f\\\"  depth {d}: {count} samples\\\")\\n    return texts, np.array(depths), np.array(doc_ids)\\n\\n\\n# ── Embedding extraction ─────────────────────────────────────────────\\n\\n@torch.no_grad()\\ndef extract_embeddings(model, tokenizer, texts, batch_size=24, max_length=512):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Frozen LLM last hidden state → average pool → (N, hidden_dim).\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    all_emb = []\\n    for i in tqdm(range(0, len(texts), batch_size), desc=\\\"Embedding\\\"):\\n        batch = texts[i : i + batch_size]\\n        inputs = tokenizer(\\n            batch,\\n            return_tensors=\\\"pt\\\",\\n            padding=True,\\n            truncation=True,\\n            max_length=max_length,\\n        ).to(model.device)\\n\\n        out = model(**inputs, output_hidden_states=True)\\n        hidden = out.hidden_states[-1]  # <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me review the exp-015 neural probe script. It's a well-structured experiment that:

1. Takes frozen Qwen2.5-1.5B embeddings (last hidden state, avg pool → 1536-dim)
2. Trains an MLP classifier (1536 → 512 → 256 → 6 classes)
3. Uses GroupKFold CV (grouped by doc_id)
4. Computes pairwise AUC for adjacent depths
5. Determines K* with the same θ=0.60 threshold
6. Compares against LightGBM baseline from features.csv


7. The embedding extraction, MLP architecture with early stopping and regularization, and GroupKFold validation all look solid. But I'm second-guessing the pairwise AUC approach—it trains separate classifiers for each adjacent depth pair rather than using a single model, which might not capture the full depth hierarchy.

8. There's also a self-scoring issue I need to flag: the probe uses embeddings from the same Qwen2.5-1.5B model that generated the text, so the model might have inherent familiarity with its own outputs that inflates the K* estimates. Testing with a different scorer model would be needed to validate whether this is actually a problem.

9. Memory-wise, the setup should be fine—5000 samples across 6 depths with 1536-dimensional embeddings comes to about 184MB, and the batch size of 24 with 512 max_length should fit comfortably on a 4090.

10. The script currently only runs on Qwen-cont data, though it could be extended to Pythia and OLMo later.

11. The baseline comparison loads hand-crafted features from the same directory and runs OBD-LightGBM, which ensures both approaches are evaluated on identical data.

12. The core question is whether the neural probe recovers K*=2 (matching the hand-crafted result), which would suggest the information boundary is inherent to the data rather than an artifact of feature engineering. If K* is higher, the hand-crafted features are limiting; if lower, the neural probe might be overfitting or the MLP underperforming compared to LightGBM.

13. The pairwise binary classification approach handles the ordinal structure appropriately for computing AUC across depth pairs, and I should verify it's using the θ=0.60 threshold consistently throughout.

14. The K* computation happens in the truncated `run_one_dataset` function, but I can confirm from the output that it's being extracted and stored properly.

15. The script is solid overall with no critical flaws—the main limitation is testing only on Qwen-cont by default, though that's the intended baseline condition.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PR35JtuUn7GRMvqzBdfGQQ`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	% === Pipeline figure placeholder ===
5	% \begin{figure}[t]
6	% \centering
7	% \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline.pdf}
8	% \caption{Overview of the generational depth estimation pipeline. Seed documents from C4 are recursively passed through a generation operator $G_\delta$ to produce a depth chain $X \to Y_1 \to \cdots \to Y_K$. Statistical features extracted by an external scorer model are fed to the OBD-LightGBM classifier for depth prediction.}
9	% \label{fig:pipeline}
10	% \end{figure}
11	
12	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
13	\label{sec:formulation}
14	
15	Recursive text generation naturally forms an iterated lossy channel.
16	Let $X$ denote an original human-written document drawn from a source distribution $\mathcal{D}$.
17	A generation operator $G_\delta = \text{LLM} + \delta$, parameterized by a decoding strategy $\delta$ (e.g., nucleus sampling with top-$p$ and temperature $T$), produces successive generations:
18	\begin{equation}
19	  X \to Y_1 \to Y_2 \to \cdots \to Y_K, \quad Y_k = G_\delta(Y_{k-1}),
20	  \label{eq:markov}
21	\end{equation}
22	where $Y_0 \triangleq X$.
23	The sequence $\{Y_k\}_{k=0}^K$ forms a Markov chain, since each generation depends only on its immediate predecessor.
24	
25	By the Data Processing Inequality (DPI), mutual information between the original document and successive generations is monotonically non-increasing:
26	\begin{equation}
27	  I(X; Y_1) \geq I(X; Y_2) \geq \cdots \geq 0.
28	  \label{eq:dpi}
29	\end{equation}
30	Since mutual information is bounded below by zero, this monotone sequence converges: the original signal degrades monotonically across generations.
31	Moreover, when the generation operator defines a finite-state Markov chain, the marginal distributions of adjacent generations converge to a shared stationary distribution, so that any classifier's ability to distinguish depth $k$ from depth $k{+}1$ must eventually degrade to chance.
32	Together, these arguments guarantee the existence of a finite $\Ktheory$ beyond which adjacent generations are indistinguishable---the \emph{information-theoretic discrimination boundary}.
33	
34	Motivated by this theoretical guarantee, we set out to \emph{empirically measure} the discrimination boundary.
35	We define an operational lower bound $\Kempirical$ via a classifier's ability to distinguish adjacent generations:
36	\begin{equation}
37	  \Kempirical = \max\{k : \text{CI}_{\text{lower}}^{95\%}(\auc_{k,k+1}) > \theta\},
38	  \label{eq:kstar}
39	\end{equation}
40	where $\auc_{k,k+1}$ is the pairwise area under the ROC curve for distinguishing depth $k$ from $k{+}1$, $\text{CI}_{\text{lower}}^{95\%}$ denotes the lower bound of a bootstrap 95\% confidence interval (1{,}000 resamples), and $\theta$ is an operational threshold justified in \S\ref{sec:kstar_operational}.
41	Since any specific classifier can only recover a subset of the information-theoretically available signal, $\Kempirical \leq \Ktheory$ by construction.
42	
43	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
44	\label{sec:features}
45	
46	We characterize each document with a 15-dimensional statistical feature vector, organized into five groups (Table~\ref{tab:features}).
47	All features are computed from token-level surprisal values and frequency statistics obtained via an external scorer model on the tokenized text.
48	
49	\begin{table}[t]
50	\centering
51	\small
52	\caption{The 15-dimensional feature set for generational depth estimation.}
53	\label{tab:features}
54	\begin{tabular}{lll}
55	\toprule
56	\textbf{Group} & \textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Description} \\
57	\midrule
58	\multirow{4}{*}{\parbox{2.2cm}{Surprisal\\moments}}
59	  & \texttt{surp\_mean}      & Mean token surprisal \\
60	  & \texttt{surp\_std}       & Std.\ dev.\ of token surprisal \\
61	  & \texttt{surp\_skew}      & Skewness of surprisal dist. \\
62	  & \texttt{surp\_kurt}      & Kurtosis of surprisal dist. \\
63	\midrule
64	\multirow{5}{*}{\parbox{2.2cm}{Surprisal\\derivatives}}
65	  & \texttt{surp\_d1\_mean}  & Mean 1st-order surprisal diff. \\
66	  & \texttt{surp\_d1\_std}   & Std.\ dev.\ of 1st-order diff. \\
67	  & \texttt{surp\_d1\_skew}  & Skewness of 1st-order diff. \\
68	  & \texttt{surp\_d2\_mean}  & Mean 2nd-order surprisal diff. \\
69	  & \texttt{surp\_d2\_std}   & Std.\ dev.\ of 2nd-order diff. \\
70	\midrule
71	\multirow{3}{*}{\parbox{2.2cm}{Vocabulary\\diversity}}
72	  & \texttt{ttr}             & Type-token ratio \\
73	  & \texttt{hapax\_ratio}    & Hapax legomena / unique tokens \\
74	  & \texttt{self\_bleu}      & 4-gram self-BLEU (within-document) \\
75	\midrule
76	\multirow{3}{*}{\parbox{2.2cm}{Frequency\\tail}}
77	  & \texttt{freq\_kurtosis}  & Kurtosis of token frequencies \\
78	  & \texttt{freq\_entropy}   & Token frequency entropy \\
79	  & \texttt{low\_freq\_ratio} & Fraction of rare tokens (freq $\leq 2$) \\
80	\bottomrule
81	\end{tabular}
82	\end{table}
83	
84	The first two groups capture the distribution and dynamics of token-level surprisal: moments summarize the shape of the surprisal distribution, while derivatives measure its local variability across token positions.
85	Vocabulary diversity features quantify lexical richness and repetitiveness: type-token ratio and hapax proportion reflect vocabulary breadth, while self-BLEU detects the increased intra-document $n$-gram overlap characteristic of deeper generations.
86	Frequency tail features characterize the token frequency distribution, capturing the well-documented phenomenon whereby recursive generation concentrates probability mass on high-frequency tokens and reduces the fraction of rare tokens \cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.
87	
88	We use hand-crafted statistical features rather than learned representations for three reasons: (1) they are directly interpretable, enabling degradation ordering analysis across model families; (2) they transfer across generators without retraining the feature extractor; and (3) they permit attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties, which is essential for understanding \emph{how} the depth signal degrades.
89	
90	\subsection{Ordinal Binary Decomposition Classifier}
91	\label{sec:classifier}
92	
93	Generational depth is inherently ordinal: depth~1 is closer to depth~0 than depth~3 is.
94	We exploit this structure via \emph{Ordinal Binary Decomposition} (OBD), which decomposes a $(K{+}1)$-class ordinal classification problem into $K$ cumulative binary sub-classifiers.
95	The $k$-th sub-classifier estimates:
96	\begin{equation}
97	  P(\text{depth} \leq k \mid \mathbf{x}), \quad k = 0, 1, \ldots, K{-}1,
98	\end{equation}
99	where $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^{15}$ is the feature vector.
100	Each sub-classifier is an independent LightGBM model.
101	The predicted depth is the smallest $k$ for which the cumulative probability exceeds 0.5:
102	\begin{equation}
103	  \hat{d}(\mathbf{x}) = \min\{k : P(\text{depth} \leq k \mid \mathbf{x}) > 0.5\}.
104	\end{equation}
105	
106	We compared OBD-LightGBM against two alternatives: standard (unordered) multiclass LightGBM and ordinal logistic regression (LogisticAT).
107	OBD-LightGBM achieves lower mean absolute error (MAE) than multiclass LightGBM across all three model families (Qwen-rewrite: 0.984 vs.\ 1.016; Pythia: 1.107 vs.\ 1.140; OLMo: 1.147 vs.\ 1.181).
108	Ordinal logistic regression yields the lowest MAE overall (e.g., 0.911 on Qwen-rewrite) but at the cost of substantially degraded pairwise discrimination: mean pairwise $\auc$ of 0.4883 versus 0.6095 for OBD-LightGBM, averaged across the three families.
109	The low MAE of ordinal logistic regression stems from collapsing predictions toward central depths rather than improved discrimination---a pathology that renders it unsuitable when pairwise depth discriminability is the primary objective.
110	
111	\subsection{Operationalizing $\Kstar$}
112	\label{sec:kstar_operational}
113	
114	We set the operational threshold at $\theta = 0.60$.
115	This choice is grounded in the permutation test null distribution: across all model configurations, the 95th percentile of the null $\auc$ (obtained from 500 random label permutations) falls below 0.55.
116	A threshold of $\theta = 0.60$ therefore requires classification performance that is statistically significantly above chance at the $\alpha = 0.05$ level.
117	
118	Uncertainty is quantified via bootstrap resampling (1{,}000 resamples) of $\auc_{k,k+1}$ for each adjacent depth pair.
119	$\Kempirical$ is defined as the maximum depth $k$ for which $\text{CI}_{\text{lower}}^{95\%}(\auc_{k,k+1}) > \theta$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:kstar}).
120	
121	We examine threshold sensitivity at $\theta \in \{0.55, 0.60, 0.65, 0.70\}$.
122	At $\theta = 0.60$, $\Kempirical = 2$ holds across all four model configurations, with all permutation $p$-values for depth pair $1$ vs.\ $2$ at $p = 0.002$.
123	At the more lenient $\theta = 0.55$, $\Kempirical$ ranges from 2 to 3 depending on the configuration.
124	At the stricter $\theta = 0.65$, only the rewrite configuration retains $\Kempirical = 2$; continuation-mode configurations yield $\Kempirical = 1$.
125	At $\theta = 0.70$, $\Kempirical = 1$ universally.
126	This sensitivity profile confirms that $\Kempirical = 2$ is robust at our chosen threshold while remaining appropriately responsive to more stringent criteria.
127	
128	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
129	\label{sec:experimental_setup}
130	
131	We evaluate four model configurations spanning three model families and two generation modes (Table~\ref{tab:setup}).
132	
133	\begin{table}[t]
134	\centering
135	\small
136	\caption{Experimental configurations. All use the same seed corpus and evaluation protocol (5-fold group cross-validation grouped by seed document, $\auc$ as primary metric).}
137	\label{tab:setup}
138	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
139	\toprule
140	\textbf{Config} & \textbf{Model} & \textbf{Mode} & \textbf{Decoding} & $N$ & \textbf{Depth} \\
141	\midrule
142	Qwen-rw   & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Rewrite      & $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$ & 5{,}000 & 0--5 \\
143	Qwen-cont & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Continuation & $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$ & 5{,}000 & 0--5 \\
144	Pythia    & Pythia-1.4B            & Continuation & $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$ & 5{,}000 & 0--5 \\
145	OLMo      & OLMo-1B               & Continuation & $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$ & 5{,}000 & 0--5 \\
146	\bottomrule
147	\end{tabular}
148	\end{table}
149	
150	\paragraph{Seed corpus.}
151	We sample 5{,}000 documents from the C4 validation split, each truncated to 256 tokens, providing a controlled and reproducible starting point.
152	
153	\paragraph{Depth chain.}
154	Each seed document is recursively passed through the generation operator for $K = 5$ iterations, producing depths 0 (original) through 5.
155	This yields 30{,}000 documents per configuration (5{,}000 seeds $\times$ 6 depth levels).
156	
157	\paragraph{Decoding.}
158	All configurations use nucleus sampling with $p = 0.95$ and temperature $T = 1.0$, which we treat as the standard decoding condition.
159	The effect of alternative decoding strategies (greedy, lower temperature, different $p$) on $\Kempirical$ is examined in a separate ablation (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
160	
161	\paragraph{Evaluation.}
162	We report pairwise $\auc$ for each adjacent depth pair $(k, k{+}1)$ under 5-fold group cross-validation (grouped by seed document to prevent cross-depth leakage), with bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals.
163	Ordinal MAE and Kendall's $\tau_b$ are reported as secondary metrics.
164	
165	\subsection{Generation Mode Control}
166	\label{sec:generation_mode}
167	
168	We investigate two generation modes that capture distinct recursive contamination scenarios:
169	
170	\paragraph{Continuation.}
171	The seed document is truncated to its first 128 tokens, and the LLM generates a continuation to 256 tokens.
172	This simulates scenarios where partial human text is extended by a model, a common pattern in collaborative writing and web content generation.
173	
174	\paragraph{Rewrite.}
175	The LLM receives the instruction ``Rewrite the following text while preserving its meaning'' and generates a full paraphrase.
176	This simulates content rewriting pipelines where existing text is rephrased at scale.
177	
178	We designate continuation as the \emph{canonical condition} for cross-model comparisons, based on three empirical findings from the cross-model transfer analysis.
179	First, classifiers trained on continuation-mode data retain 80--92\% accuracy when transferred across model families, versus only 61--63\% for rewrite-mode classifiers.
180	Second, the higher pairwise $\auc$ observed in rewrite mode ($\auc_{1,2} = 0.6955$ vs.\ $0.6112$ for continuation on Qwen) reflects instruction-following style artifacts rather than a stronger intrinsic depth signal, as evidenced by the poor cross-model transfer.
181	Third, the asymmetric transfer---continuation${\to}$rewrite retains 89\% performance versus rewrite${\to}$continuation at 82\%---indicates that continuation-mode features form a generalizable subset of what rewrite-mode classifiers exploit.
182	We therefore report continuation-mode results as primary and treat rewrite-mode results as an upper-bound ablation that quantifies the effect of mode-specific signal amplification.
183	
```

Seed documents from C4 are recursively passed through a generation operator $G_\\delta$ to produce a depth chain $X \\to Y_1 \\to \\cdots \\to Y_K$. Statistical features extracted by an external scorer model are fed to the OBD-LightGBM classifier for depth prediction.}\n% \\label{fig:pipeline}\n% \\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\nRecursive text generation naturally forms an iterated lossy channel.\nLet $X$ denote an original human-written document drawn from a source distribution $\\mathcal{D}$.\nA generation operator $G_\\delta = \\text{LLM} + \\delta$, parameterized by a decoding strategy $\\delta$ (e.g., nucleus sampling with top-$p$ and temperature $T$), produces successive generations:\n\\begin{equation}\n  X \\to Y_1 \\to Y_2 \\to \\cdots \\to Y_K, \\quad Y_k = G_\\delta(Y_{k-1}),\n  \\label{eq:markov}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $Y_0 \\triangleq X$.\nThe sequence $\\{Y_k\\}_{k=0}^K$ forms a Markov chain, since each generation depends only on its immediate predecessor.\n\nBy the Data Processing Inequality (DPI), mutual information between the original document and successive generations is monotonically non-increasing:\n\\begin{equation}\n  I(X; Y_1) \\geq I(X; Y_2) \\geq \\cdots \\geq 0.\n  \\label{eq:dpi}\n\\end{equation}\nSince mutual information is bounded below by zero, this monotone sequence converges: the original signal degrades monotonically across generations.\nMoreover, when the generation operator defines a finite-state Markov chain, the marginal distributions of adjacent generations converge to a shared stationary distribution, so that any classifier's ability to distinguish depth $k$ from depth $k{+}1$ must eventually degrade to chance.\nTogether, these arguments guarantee the existence of a finite $\\Ktheory$ beyond which adjacent generations are indistinguishable---the \\emph{information-theoretic discrimination boundary}.\n\nMotivated by this theoretical guarantee, we set out to \\emph{empirically measure} the discrimination boundary.\nWe define an operational lower bound $\\Kempirical$ via a classifier's ability to distinguish adjacent generations:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\Kempirical = \\max\\{k : \\text{CI}_{\\text{lower}}^{95\\%}(\\auc_{k,k+1}) > \\theta\\},\n  \\label{eq:kstar}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\auc_{k,k+1}$ is the pairwise area under the ROC curve for distinguishing depth $k$ from $k{+}1$, $\\text{CI}_{\\text{lower}}^{95\\%}$ denotes the lower bound of a bootstrap 95\\% confidence interval (1{,}000 resamples), and $\\theta$ is an operational threshold justified in \\S\\ref{sec:kstar_operational}.\nSince any specific classifier can only recover a subset of the information-theoretically available signal, $\\Kempirical \\leq \\Ktheory$ by construction.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\nWe characterize each document with a 15-dimensional statistical feature vector, organized into five groups (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\nAll features are computed from token-level surprisal values and frequency statistics obtained via an external scorer model on the tokenized text.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 15-dimensional feature set for generational depth estimation.}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{lll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Group} & \\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{Description} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\parbox{2.2cm}{Surprisal\\\\moments}}\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_mean}      & Mean token surprisal \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_std}       & Std.\\ dev.\\ of token surprisal \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_skew}      & Skewness of surprisal dist. \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_kurt}      & Kurtosis of surprisal dist. \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{5}{*}{\\parbox{2.2cm}{Surprisal\\\\derivatives}}\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_mean}  & Mean 1st-order surprisal diff. \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}   & Std.\\ dev.\\ of 1st-order diff. \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_skew}  & Skewness of 1st-order diff. \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_mean}  & Mean 2nd-order surprisal diff. \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}   & Std.\\ dev.\\ of 2nd-order diff. \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\parbox{2.2cm}{Vocabulary\\\\diversity}}\n  & \\texttt{ttr}             & Type-token ratio \\\\\n  & \\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}    & Hapax legomena / unique tokens \\\\\n  & \\texttt{self\\_bleu}      & 4-gram self-BLEU (within-document) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\parbox{2.2cm}{Frequency\\\\tail}}\n  & \\texttt{freq\\_kurtosis}  & Kurtosis of token frequencies \\\\\n  & \\texttt{freq\\_entropy}   & Token frequency entropy \\\\\n  & \\texttt{low\\_freq\\_ratio} & Fraction of rare tokens (freq $\\leq 2$) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe first two groups capture the distribution and dynamics of token-level surprisal: moments summarize the shape of the surprisal distribution, while derivatives measure its local variability across token positions.\nVocabulary diversity features quantify lexical richness and repetitiveness: type-token ratio and hapax proportion reflect vocabulary breadth, while self-BLEU detects the increased intra-document $n$-gram overlap characteristic of deeper generations.\nFrequency tail features characterize the token frequency distribution, capturing the well-documented phenomenon whereby recursive generation concentrates probability mass on high-frequency tokens and reduces the fraction of rare tokens \\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.\n\nWe use hand-crafted statistical features rather than learned representations for three reasons: (1) they are directly interpretable, enabling degradation ordering analysis across model families; (2) they transfer across generators without retraining the feature extractor; and (3) they permit attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties, which is essential for understanding \\emph{how} the depth signal degrades.\n\n\\subsection{Ordinal Binary Decomposition Classifier}\n\\label{sec:classifier}\n\nGenerational depth is inherently ordinal: depth~1 is closer to depth~0 than depth~3 is.\nWe exploit this structure via \\emph{Ordinal Binary Decomposition} (OBD), which decomposes a $(K{+}1)$-class ordinal classification problem into $K$ cumulative binary sub-classifiers.\nThe $k$-th sub-classifier estimates:\n\\begin{equation}\n  P(\\text{depth} \\leq k \\mid \\mathbf{x}), \\quad k = 0, 1, \\ldots, K{-}1,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{x} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{15}$ is the feature vector.\nEach sub-classifier is an independent LightGBM model.\nThe predicted depth is the smallest $k$ for which the cumulative probability exceeds 0.5:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\hat{d}(\\mathbf{x}) = \\min\\{k : P(\\text{depth} \\leq k \\mid \\mathbf{x}) > 0.5\\}.\n\\end{equation}\n\nWe compared OBD-LightGBM against two alternatives: standard (unordered) multiclass LightGBM and ordinal logistic regression (LogisticAT).\nOBD-LightGBM achieves lower mean absolute error (MAE) than multiclass LightGBM across all three model families (Qwen-rewrite: 0.984 vs.\\ 1.016; Pythia: 1.107 vs.\\ 1.140; OLMo: 1.147 vs.\\ 1.181).\nOrdinal logistic regression yields the lowest MAE overall (e.g., 0.911 on Qwen-rewrite) but at the cost of substantially degraded pairwise discrimination: mean pairwise $\\auc$ of 0.4883 versus 0.6095 for OBD-LightGBM, averaged across the three families.\nThe low MAE of ordinal logistic regression stems from collapsing predictions toward central depths rather than improved discrimination---a pathology that renders it unsuitable when pairwise depth discriminability is the primary objective.\n\n\\subsection{Operationalizing $\\Kstar$}\n\\label{sec:kstar_operational}\n\nWe set the operational threshold at $\\theta = 0.60$.\nThis choice is grounded in the permutation test null distribution: across all model configurations, the 95th percentile of the null $\\auc$ (obtained from 500 random label permutations) falls below 0.55.\nA threshold of $\\theta = 0.60$ therefore requires classification performance that is statistically significantly above chance at the $\\alpha = 0.05$ level.\n\nUncertainty is quantified via bootstrap resampling (1{,}000 resamples) of $\\auc_{k,k+1}$ for each adjacent depth pair.\n$\\Kempirical$ is defined as the maximum depth $k$ for which $\\text{CI}_{\\text{lower}}^{95\\%}(\\auc_{k,k+1}) > \\theta$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:kstar}).\n\nWe examine threshold sensitivity at $\\theta \\in \\{0.55, 0.60, 0.65, 0.70\\}$.\nAt $\\theta = 0.60$, $\\Kempirical = 2$ holds across all four model configurations, with all permutation $p$-values for depth pair $1$ vs.\\ $2$ at $p = 0.002$.\nAt the more lenient $\\theta = 0.55$, $\\Kempirical$ ranges from 2 to 3 depending on the configuration.\nAt the stricter $\\theta = 0.65$, only the rewrite configuration retains $\\Kempirical = 2$; continuation-mode configurations yield $\\Kempirical = 1$.\nAt $\\theta = 0.70$, $\\Kempirical = 1$ universally.\nThis sensitivity profile confirms that $\\Kempirical = 2$ is robust at our chosen threshold while remaining appropriately responsive to more stringent criteria.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate four model configurations spanning three model families and two generation modes (Table~\\ref{tab:setup}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Experimental configurations.
```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Web-scale text corpora are increasingly contaminated by synthetic content that has itself been generated from earlier synthetic content.
5	Each pass through a language model constitutes a lossy channel: first-generation synthetic text retains much of the information structure of its human-written source, but by the fourth generation the output is qualitatively different---tail modes vanish, lexical diversity contracts, and surprisal distributions flatten~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.
6	This recursive degradation is not merely a theoretical concern; as synthetic text proliferates on the open web, training corpora inevitably ingest documents at varying generational depths, amplifying the risk of model collapse in downstream systems.
7	Yet the dominant paradigm in synthetic text detection treats all machine-generated text identically: a document is classified as human or synthetic, with no regard for how many rounds of recursive generation it has undergone.
8	This binary framing discards the generational depth dimension entirely.
9	
10	The gap cuts across three research communities.
11	Binary detection methods---including DivEye~\cite{basani2026diveye}, Min-K\%++~\cite{zhang2024minkpp}, and CoDeC~\cite{zawalski2026codec}---achieve strong human-versus-machine separation but produce no depth estimate.
12	The model collapse literature provides macro-level characterizations of recursive degradation: Shumailov et al.~\cite{shumailov2024collapse} demonstrated irreversible tail loss, Alemohammad et al.~\cite{alemohammad2024selfconsuming} proved convergence to a fixed point, and Schaeffer et al.~\cite{schaeffer2025position} organized the field into a taxonomy of eight definitions---yet none of these works provides a tool for estimating the generational depth of an individual text.
13	Data selection methods such as DSIR~\cite{xie2023dsir}, D4~\cite{tirumala2024d4}, and DoReMi~\cite{xie2024doremi} perform distribution matching to improve corpus quality but ignore recursive depth altogether.
14	The closest prior work is Drayson et al.~\cite{drayson2025}, who combined binary machine-generated text detection with stratified importance resampling to prevent model collapse---but their approach remains fundamentally binary.
15	No existing method estimates how many rounds of recursive generation a text has undergone, nor quantifies the boundary beyond which such estimation becomes infeasible.
16	
17	Recursive generation forms an iterated lossy channel; the Data Processing Inequality guarantees that the original signal degrades monotonically across generations, motivating the existence of a finite discrimination boundary $\Kstar$ beyond which adjacent generations become indistinguishable.
18	Markov chain convergence further implies that adjacent-generation distributions converge to a shared stationary distribution, so the classifier's task grows harder with each step.
19	Motivated by this theoretical structure, we set out to empirically measure $\Kempirical$---the maximum depth at which a classifier can reliably distinguish adjacent generations.
20	Our approach combines a 15-dimensional statistical feature pipeline---capturing surprisal moments, surprisal derivatives, vocabulary diversity, and frequency tail statistics---with an Ordinal Binary Decomposition (OBD) LightGBM classifier that respects the ordinal structure of depth.
21	We operationalize $\Kempirical$ via a bootstrap confidence interval protocol: $\Kempirical = k$ if the 95\% CI lower bound of $\auc_{k,k+1}$ exceeds $\theta = 0.60$ and the CI upper bound of $\auc_{k+1,k+2}$ falls below $\theta$.
22	The choice of interpretable statistical features---rather than learned representations---is deliberate: it enables cross-generator transfer without retraining and permits attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties.
23	
24	Our contributions are organized into two groups.
25	The primary contribution is a systematic empirical characterization of $\Kempirical$ and its properties:
26	
27	\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
28	  \item \textbf{$\Kempirical = 2$ across all tested configurations.}
29	    Across 4 configurations spanning 3 model families and 2 generation modes, all 1v2 bootstrap CI lower bounds exceed $\theta = 0.60$ and all 2v3 CI upper bounds fall below $\theta$, with permutation $p \leq 0.002$ for all boundary comparisons.
30	    The most marginal case is Qwen-cont, where $\text{CI}_\text{lower} = 0.6015$.
31	    Preliminary results from greedy decoding also yield $\Kempirical = 2$, consistent with the hypothesis that the discrimination boundary is robust to decoding strategy; full ablation results are reported in Section~\ref{sec:experiments}.
32	
33	  \item \textbf{Cross-model transfer.}
34	    Classifiers trained on one continuation-mode model and evaluated on another retain 80--92\% of within-model accuracy (Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 91--92\%).
35	    At the 0v1 boundary, cross-model $\auc$ exceeds 0.96 universally.
36	    Transfer retention of 80--92\% suggests a large text-intrinsic component of the depth signal, rather than generator-specific artifacts.
37	
38	  \item \textbf{Degradation ordering consistency.}
39	    Single-feature $\auc$ rankings are highly correlated across continuation-mode models (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.85$), with the same top features---\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}, \texttt{surp\_std}, \texttt{surp\_d2\_std}---appearing across all three families.
40	    This shared ordering suggests a common degradation mechanism: each generation step smooths the token-level surprisal landscape.
41	    A limitation is that depth-pair-level agreement weakens ($\tau \approx 0.37$ at 1v2), indicating that individual feature contributions at the boundary vary across models.
42	
43	  \item \textbf{Generation mode analysis.}
44	    Within Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, rewrite mode yields 1v2 $\auc = 0.6955$ versus continuation's $0.6112$ ($\Delta = +8.4$\,pp), but rewrite-trained classifiers transfer poorly across models (61--63\% retention versus 80--92\% for continuation).
45	    Cross-mode transfer is asymmetric: continuation$\to$rewrite retains 89\% while rewrite$\to$continuation retains 82\%.
46	    Continuation mode is therefore recommended as the canonical condition for cross-model comparisons.
47	\end{enumerate}
48	
49	\noindent As a secondary contribution, we investigate whether the coarse 3-class ordinal signal implied by $\Kempirical = 2$ enables \emph{graduated filtering}---depth-aware data curation that preserves mildly contaminated text (depth~1, retaining most original signal) while removing deeply corrupted text (depth~$\geq 3$)---and whether such filtering can improve upon binary approaches that discard all synthetic content indiscriminately (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
50	
51	The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
52	Section~\ref{sec:related_work} reviews related work.
53	Section~\ref{sec:method} describes the method.
54	Section~\ref{sec:experiments} presents experimental results.
55	Section~5 concludes.
```

\\textbf{$\\Kempirical = 2$ across all tested configurations.}\n    Across 4 configurations spanning 3 model families and 2 generation modes, all 1v2 bootstrap CI lower bounds exceed $\\theta = 0.60$ and all 2v3 CI upper bounds fall below $\\theta$, with permutation $p \\leq 0.002$ for all boundary comparisons.\n    The most marginal case is Qwen-cont, where $\\text{CI}_\\text{lower} = 0.6015$.\n    Preliminary results from greedy decoding also yield $\\Kempirical = 2$, consistent with the hypothesis that the discrimination boundary is robust to decoding strategy; full ablation results are reported in Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Cross-model transfer.}\n    Classifiers trained on one continuation-mode model and evaluated on another retain 80--92\\% of within-model accuracy (Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 91--92\\%).\n    At the 0v1 boundary, cross-model $\\auc$ exceeds 0.96 universally.\n    Transfer retention of 80--92\\% suggests a large text-intrinsic component of the depth signal, rather than generator-specific artifacts.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Degradation ordering consistency.}\n    Single-feature $\\auc$ rankings are highly correlated across continuation-mode models (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.85$), with the same top features---\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}---appearing across all three families.\n    This shared ordering suggests a common degradation mechanism: each generation step smooths the token-level surprisal landscape.\n    A limitation is that depth-pair-level agreement weakens ($\\tau \\approx 0.37$ at 1v2), indicating that individual feature contributions at the boundary vary across models.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Generation mode analysis.}\n    Within Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, rewrite mode yields 1v2 $\\auc = 0.6955$ versus continuation's $0.6112$ ($\\Delta = +8.4$\\,pp), but rewrite-trained classifiers transfer poorly across models (61--63\\% retention versus 80--92\\% for continuation).\n    Cross-mode transfer is asymmetric: continuation$\\to$rewrite retains 89\\% while rewrite$\\to$continuation retains 82\\%.\n    Continuation mode is therefore recommended as the canonical condition for cross-model comparisons.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\noindent As a secondary contribution, we investigate whether the coarse 3-class ordinal signal implied by $\\Kempirical = 2$ enables \\emph{graduated filtering}---depth-aware data curation that preserves mildly contaminated text (depth~1, retaining most original signal) while removing deeply corrupted text (depth~$\\geq 3$)---and whether such filtering can improve upon binary approaches that discard all synthetic content indiscriminately (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows.\nSection~\\ref{sec:related_work} reviews related work.\nSection~\\ref{sec:method} describes the method.\nSection~\\ref{sec:experiments} presents experimental results.\nSection~5 concludes.",

I operate in read-only mode — no experiment execution, no file modification. All output must be in Chinese. The project studies K* (generational discrimination boundary) for recursive synthetic text, targeting EMNLP 2026 Main submission.

   In this session specifically, I:

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **K* (Saturation Boundary)**: Maximum depth at which adjacent generational depths can be distinguished (AUC > θ=0.60). Measured as K*=2 across all tested conditions.
   - **FEATURE_COLS (15D)**: surp_mean, surp_std, surp_skew, surp_kurt, surp_d1_mean, surp_d1_std, surp_d1_skew, surp_d2_mean, surp_d2_std, ttr, hapax_ratio, self_bleu, freq_kurtosis, freq_entropy, low_freq_ratio
   - **GroupKFold CV**: Code uses GroupKFold (grouped by seed document), correctly described in method.tex
   - **OBD-LightGBM**: Ordinal Binary Decomposition with K-1 binary classifiers for P(depth>k)
   - **DPI argument**: I(X;Y_k) monotonically decreasing; combined with Markov chain convergence for K* existence
   - **Bootstrap CI**: 1000-iteration bootstrap with doc-level resampling. Most marginal: Qwen-cont CI lower=0.6015
   - **Server rename**: autodl-4090d → westc-26893 (same physical machine, caused monitoring false positive)
   - **Generation modes**: "rewrite" (instruct prompt, default) vs "continuation" (truncate+continue). Continuation = canonical condition.

```json
{
```

```json
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWeb-scale text corpora are increasingly contaminated by synthetic content that has itself been generated from earlier synthetic content.\nEach pass through a language model constitutes a lossy channel: first-generation synthetic text retains much of the information structure of its human-written source, but by the fourth generation the output is qualitatively different---tail modes vanish, lexical diversity contracts, and surprisal distributions flatten~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.\nThis recursive degradation is not merely a theoretical concern; as synthetic text proliferates on the open web, training corpora inevitably ingest documents at varying generational depths, amplifying the risk of model collapse in downstream systems.\nYet the dominant paradigm in synthetic text detection treats all machine-generated text identically: a document is classified as human or synthetic, with no regard for how many rounds of recursive generation it has undergone.\nThis binary framing discards the generational depth dimension entirely.\n\nThe gap cuts across three research communities.\nBinary detection methods---including DivEye~\\cite{basani2026diveye}, Min-K\\%++~\\cite{zhang2024minkpp}, and CoDeC~\\cite{zawalski2026codec}---achieve strong human-versus-machine separation but produce no depth estimate.\nThe model collapse literature provides macro-level characterizations of recursive degradation: Shumailov et al.~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse} demonstrated irreversible tail loss, Alemohammad et al.~\\cite{alemohammad2024selfconsuming} proved convergence to a fixed point, and Schaeffer et al.~\\cite{schaeffer2025position} organized the field into a taxonomy of eight definitions---yet none of these works provides a tool for estimating the generational depth of an individual text.\nData selection methods such as DSIR~\\cite{xie2023dsir}, D4~\\cite{tirumala2024d4}, and DoReMi~\\cite{xie2024doremi} perform distribution matching to improve corpus quality but ignore recursive depth altogether.\nThe closest prior work is Drayson et al.~\\cite{drayson2025}, who combined binary machine-generated text detection with stratified importance resampling to prevent model collapse---but their approach remains fundamentally binary.\nNo existing method estimates how many rounds of recursive generation a text has undergone, nor quantifies the boundary beyond which such estimation becomes infeasible.\n\nRecursive generation forms an iterated lossy channel; the Data Processing Inequality guarantees that the original signal degrades monotonically across generations, motivating the existence of a finite discrimination boundary $\\Kstar$ beyond which adjacent generations become indistinguishable.\nMarkov chain convergence further implies that adjacent-generation distributions converge to a shared stationary distribution, so the classifier's task grows harder with each step.\nMotivated by this theoretical structure, we set out to empirically measure $\\Kempirical$---the maximum depth at which a classifier can reliably distinguish adjacent generations.\nOur approach combines a 15-dimensional statistical feature pipeline---capturing surprisal moments, surprisal derivatives, vocabulary diversity, and frequency tail statistics---with an Ordinal Binary Decomposition (OBD) LightGBM classifier that respects the ordinal structure of depth.\nWe operationalize $\\Kempirical$ via a bootstrap confidence interval protocol: $\\Kempirical = k$ if the 95\\% CI lower bound of $\\auc_{k,k+1}$ exceeds $\\theta = 0.60$ and the CI upper bound of $\\auc_{k+1,k+2}$ falls below $\\theta$.\nThe choice of interpretable statistical features---rather than learned representations---is deliberate: it enables cross-generator transfer without retraining and permits attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties.\n\nOur contributions are organized into two groups.\nThe primary contribution is a systematic empirical characterization of $\\Kempirical$ and its properties:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=(\\alph*)]\n  \\item \\textbf{$\\Kempirical = 2$ across all tested configurations.}\n    Across 4 configurations spanning 3 model families and 2 generation modes, all 1v2 bootstrap CI lower bounds exceed $\\theta = 0.60$ and all 2v3 CI upper bounds fall below $\\theta$, with permutation $p \\leq 0.002$ for all boundary comparisons.\n    The most marginal case is Qwen-cont, where $\\text{CI}_\\text{lower} = 0.6015$.\n    Preliminary results from greedy decoding also yield $\\Kempirical = 2$, consistent with the hypothesis that the discrimination boundary is robust to decoding strategy; full ablation results are reported in Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Cross-model transfer.}\n    Classifiers trained on one continuation-mode model and evaluated on another retain 80--92\\% of within-model accuracy (Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 91--92\\%).\n    At the 0v1 boundary, cross-model $\\auc$ exceeds 0.96 universally.\n    Transfer retention of 80--92\\% suggests a large text-intrinsic component of the depth signal, rather than generator-specific artifacts.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Degradation ordering consistency.}\n    Single-feature $\\auc$ rankings are highly correlated across continuation-mode models (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.85$), with the same top features---\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}---appearing across all three families.\n    This shared ordering suggests a common degradation mechanism: each generation step smooths the token-level surprisal landscape.\n    A limitation is that depth-pair-level agreement weakens ($\\tau \\approx 0.37$ at 1v2), indicating that individual feature contributions at the boundary vary across models.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Generation mode analysis.}\n    Within Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, rewrite mode yields 1v2 $\\auc = 0.6955$ versus continuation's $0.6112$ ($\\Delta = +8.4$\\,pp), but rewrite-trained classifiers transfer poorly across models (61--63\\% retention versus 80--92\\% for continuation).\n    Cross-mode transfer is asymmetric: continuation$\\to$rewrite retains 89\\% while rewrite$\\to$continuation retains 82\\%.\n    Continuation mode is therefore recommended as the canonical condition for cross-model comparisons.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\noindent As a secondary contribution, we investigate whether the coarse 3-class ordinal signal implied by $\\Kempirical = 2$ enables \\emph{graduated filtering}---depth-aware data curation that preserves mildly contaminated text (depth~1, retaining most original signal) while removing deeply corrupted text (depth~$\\geq 3$)---and whether such filtering can improve upon binary approaches that discard all synthetic content indiscriminately (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows.\nSection~\\ref{sec:related_work} reviews related work.\nSection~\\ref{sec:method} describes the method.\nSection~\\ref{sec:experiments} presents experimental results.\nSection~5 concludes.",
        "numLines": 55,

        "totalLines": 55
      }
    },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "3697eb4c-c593-4818-b347-584d05c3b3df",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n% === Pipeline figure placeholder ===\n% \\begin{figure}[t]\n% \\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline.pdf}\n% \\caption{Overview of the generational depth estimation pipeline. Seed documents from C4 are recursively passed through a generation operator $G_\\delta$ to produce a depth chain $X \\to Y_1 \\to \\cdots \\to Y_K$. Statistical features extracted by an external scorer model are fed to the OBD-LightGBM classifier for depth prediction.}\n% \\label{fig:pipeline}\n% \\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\nRecursive text generation naturally forms an iterated lossy channel.\nLet $X$ denote an original human-written document drawn from a source distribution $\\mathcal{D}$.\nA generation operator $G_\\delta = \\text{LLM} + \\delta$, parameterized by a decoding strategy $\\delta$ (e.g., nucleus sampling with top-$p$ and temperature $T$), produces successive generations:\n\\begin{equation}\n  X \\to Y_1 \\to Y_2 \\to \\cdots \\to Y_K, \\quad Y_k = G_\\delta(Y_{k-1}),\n  \\label{eq:markov}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $Y_0 \\triangleq X$.\nThe sequence $\\{Y_k\\}_{k=0}^K$ forms a Markov chain, since each generation depends only on its immediate predecessor.\n\nBy the Data Processing Inequality (DPI), mutual information between the original document and successive generations is monotonically non-increasing:\n\\begin{equation}\n  I(X; Y_1) \\geq I(X; Y_2) \\geq \\cdots \\geq 0.\n  \\label{eq:dpi}\n\\end{equation}\nSince mutual information is bounded below by zero, this monotone sequence converges: the original signal degrades monotonically across generations.\nMoreover, when the generation operator defines a finite-state Markov chain, the marginal distributions of adjacent generations converge to a shared stationary distribution, so that any classifier's ability to distinguish depth $k$ from depth $k{+}1$ must eventually degrade to chance.\nTogether, these arguments guarantee the existence of a finite $\\Ktheory$ beyond which adjacent generations are indistinguishable---the \\emph{information-theoretic discrimination boundary}.\n\nMotivated by this theoretical guarantee, we set out to \\emph{empirically measure} the discrimination boundary.\nWe define an operational lower bound $\\Kempirical$ via a classifier's ability to distinguish adjacent generations:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\Kempirical = \\max\\{k : \\text{CI}_{\\text{lower}}^{95\\%}(\\auc_{k,k+1}) > \\theta\\},\n  \\label{eq:kstar}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\auc_{k,k+1}$ is the pairwise area under the ROC curve for distinguishing depth $k$ from $k{+}1$, $\\text{CI}_{\\text{lower}}^{95\\%}$ denotes the lower bound of a bootstrap 95\\% confidence interval (1{,}000 resamples), and $\\theta$ is an operational threshold justified in \\S\\ref{sec:kstar_operational}.\nSince any specific classifier can only recover a subset of the information-theoretically available signal, $\\Kempirical \\leq \\Ktheory$ by construction.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\nWe characterize each document with a 15-dimensional statistical feature vector, organized into five groups (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\nAll features are computed from token-level surprisal values and frequency statistics obtained via an external scorer model on the tokenized text.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 15-dimensional feature set for generational depth estimation.}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{lll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Group} & \\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{Description} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\parbox{2.2cm}{Surprisal\\\\moments}}\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_mean}      & Mean token surprisal \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_std}       & Std.\\ dev.\\ of token surprisal \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_skew}      & Skewness of surprisal dist. \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_kurt}      & Kurtosis of surprisal dist. \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{5}{*}{\\parbox{2.2cm}{Surprisal\\\\derivatives}}\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_mean}  & Mean 1st-order surprisal diff. \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}   & Std.\\ dev.\\ of 1st-order diff. \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_skew}  & Skewness of 1st-order diff. \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_mean}  & Mean 2nd-order surprisal diff. \\\\\n  & \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}   & Std.\\ dev.\\ of 2nd-order diff. \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\parbox{2.2cm}{Vocabulary\\\\diversity}}\n  & \\texttt{ttr}             & Type-token ratio \\\\\n  & \\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}    & Hapax legomena / unique tokens \\\\\n  & \\texttt{self\\_bleu}      & 4-gram self-BLEU (within-document) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\parbox{2.2cm}{Frequency\\\\tail}}\n  & \\texttt{freq\\_kurtosis}  & Kurtosis of token frequencies \\\\\n  & \\texttt{freq\\_entropy}   & Token frequency entropy \\\\\n  & \\texttt{low\\_freq\\_ratio} & Fraction of rare tokens (freq $\\leq 2$) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe first two groups capture the distribution and dynamics of token-level surprisal: moments summarize the shape of the surprisal distribution, while derivatives measure its local variability across token positions.\nVocabulary diversity features quantify lexical richness and repetitiveness: type-token ratio and hapax proportion reflect vocabulary breadth, while self-BLEU detects the increased intra-document $n$-gram overlap characteristic of deeper generations.\nFrequency tail features characterize the token frequency distribution, capturing the well-documented phenomenon whereby recursive generation concentrates probability mass on high-frequency tokens and reduces the fraction of rare tokens \\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.\n\nWe use hand-crafted statistical features rather than learned representations for three reasons: (1) they are directly interpretable, enabling degradation ordering analysis across model families; (2) they transfer across generators without retraining the feature extractor; and (3) they permit attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties, which is essential for understanding \\emph{how} the depth signal degrades.\n\n\\subsection{Ordinal Binary Decomposition Classifier}\n\\label{sec:classifier}\n\nGenerational depth is inherently ordinal: depth~1 is closer to depth~0 than depth~3 is.\nWe exploit this structure via \\emph{Ordinal Binary Decomposition} (OBD), which decomposes a $(K{+}1)$-class ordinal classification problem into $K$ cumulative binary sub-classifiers.\nThe $k$-th sub-classifier estimates:\n\\begin{equation}\n  P(\\text{depth} \\leq k \\mid \\mathbf{x}), \\quad k = 0, 1, \\ldots, K{-}1,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{x} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{15}$ is the feature vector.\nEach sub-classifier is an independent LightGBM model.\nThe predicted depth is the smallest $k$ for which the cumulative probability exceeds 0.5:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\hat{d}(\\mathbf{x}) = \\min\\{k : P(\\text{depth} \\leq k \\mid \\mathbf{x}) > 0.5\\}.\n\\end{equation}\n\nWe compared OBD-LightGBM against two alternatives: standard (unordered) multiclass LightGBM and ordinal logistic regression (LogisticAT).\nOBD-LightGBM achieves lower mean absolute error (MAE) than multiclass LightGBM across all three model families (Qwen-rewrite: 0.984 vs.\\ 1.016; Pythia: 1.107 vs.\\ 1.140; OLMo: 1.147 vs.\\ 1.181).\nOrdinal logistic regression yields the lowest MAE overall (e.g., 0.911 on Qwen-rewrite) but at the cost of substantially degraded pairwise discrimination: mean pairwise $\\auc$ of 0.4883 versus 0.6095 for OBD-LightGBM, averaged across the three families.\nThe low MAE of ordinal logistic regression stems from collapsing predictions toward central depths rather than improved discrimination---a pathology that renders it unsuitable when pairwise depth discriminability is the primary objective.\n\n\\subsection{Operationalizing $\\Kstar$}\n\\label{sec:kstar_operational}\n\nWe set the operational threshold at $\\theta = 0.60$.\nThis choice is grounded in the permutation test null distribution: across all model configurations, the 95th percentile of the null $\\auc$ (obtained from 500 random label permutations) falls below 0.55.\nA threshold of $\\theta = 0.60$ therefore requires classification performance that is statistically significantly above chance at the $\\alpha = 0.05$ level.\n\nUncertainty is quantified via bootstrap resampling (1{,}000 resamples) of $\\auc_{k,k+1}$ for each adjacent depth pair.\n$\\Kempirical$ is defined as the maximum depth $k$ for which $\\text{CI}_{\\text{lower}}^{95\\%}(\\auc_{k,k+1}) > \\theta$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:kstar}).\n\nWe examine threshold sensitivity at $\\theta \\in \\{0.55, 0.60, 0.65, 0.70\\}$.\nAt $\\theta = 0.60$, $\\Kempirical = 2$ holds across all four model configurations, with all permutation $p$-values for depth pair $1$ vs.\\ $2$ at $p = 0.002$.\nAt the more lenient $\\theta = 0.55$, $\\Kempirical$ ranges from 2 to 3 depending on the configuration.\nAt the stricter $\\theta = 0.65$, only the rewrite configuration retains $\\Kempirical = 2$; continuation-mode configurations yield $\\Kempirical = 1$.\nAt $\\theta = 0.70$, $\\Kempirical = 1$ universally.\nThis sensitivity profile confirms that $\\Kempirical = 2$ is robust at our chosen threshold while remaining appropriately responsive to more stringent criteria.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate four model configurations spanning three model families and two generation modes (Table~\\ref{tab:setup}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Experimental configurations. All use the same seed corpus and evaluation protocol (5-fold group cross-validation grouped by seed document, $\\auc$ as primary metric).}\n\\label{tab:setup}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{Decoding} & $N$ & \\textbf{Depth} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-rw   & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Rewrite      & $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$ & 5{,}000 & 0--5 \\\\\nQwen-cont & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Continuation & $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$ & 5{,}000 & 0--5 \\\\\nPythia    & Pythia-1.4B            & Continuation & $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$ & 5{,}000 & 0--5 \\\\\nOLMo      & OLMo-1B               & Continuation & $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$ & 5{,}000 & 0--5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Seed corpus.}\nWe sample 5{,}000 documents from the C4 validation split, each truncated to 256 tokens, providing a controlled and reproducible starting point.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth chain.}\nEach seed document is recursively passed through the generation operator for $K = 5$ iterations, producing depths 0 (original) through 5.\nThis yields 30{,}000 documents per configuration (5{,}000 seeds $\\times$ 6 depth levels).\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding.}\nAll configurations use nucleus sampling with $p = 0.95$ and temperature $T = 1.0$, which we treat as the standard decoding condition.\nThe effect of alternative decoding strategies (greedy, lower temperature, different $p$) on $\\Kempirical$ is examined in a separate ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation.}\nWe report pairwise $\\auc$ for each adjacent depth pair $(k, k{+}1)$ under 5-fold group cross-validation (grouped by seed document to prevent cross-depth leakage), with bootstrap 95\\% confidence intervals.\nOrdinal MAE and Kendall's $\\tau_b$ are reported as secondary metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Generation Mode Control}\n\\label{sec:generation_mode}\n\nWe investigate two generation modes that capture distinct recursive contamination scenarios:\n\n\\paragraph{Continuation.}\nThe seed document is truncated to its first 128 tokens, and the LLM generates a continuation to 256 tokens.\nThis simulates scenarios where partial human text is extended by a model, a common pattern in collaborative writing and web content generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Rewrite.}\nThe LLM receives the instruction ``Rewrite the following text while preserving its meaning'' and generates a full paraphrase.\nThis simulates content rewriting pipelines where existing text is rephrased at scale.\n\nWe designate continuation as the \\emph{canonical condition} for cross-model comparisons, based on three empirical findings from the cross-model transfer analysis.\nFirst, classifiers trained on continuation-mode data retain 80--92\\% accuracy when transferred across model families, versus only 61--63\\% for rewrite-mode classifiers.\nSecond, the higher pairwise $\\auc$ observed in rewrite mode ($\\auc_{1,2} = 0.6955$ vs.\\ $0.6112$ for continuation on Qwen) reflects instruction-following style artifacts rather than a stronger intrinsic depth signal, as evidenced by the poor cross-model transfer.\nThird, the asymmetric transfer---continuation${\\to}$rewrite retains 89\\% performance versus rewrite${\\to}$continuation at 82\\%---indicates that continuation-mode features form a generalizable subset of what rewrite-mode classifiers exploit.\nWe therefore report continuation-mode results as primary and treat rewrite-mode results as an upper-bound ablation that quantifies the effect of mode-specific signal amplification.\n",
        "numLines": 183,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 183
      }
    },
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
I need to post my final quality review of introduction.tex and method.tex via `reviewer_post_to_director`. I have all the findings ready from my analysis. Let me compile and post the review.

Let me first check the K* definition inconsistency I noted - line 21 of introduction.tex mentions "CI upper bound of AUC_{k+1,k+2} falls below θ" while method.tex Eq. 3 only uses CI_lower > θ. Let me verify this carefully.

From introduction.tex line 21:
"$\Kempirical = k$ if the 95\% CI lower bound of $\auc_{k,k+1}$ exceeds $\theta = 0.60$ and the CI upper bound of $\auc_{k+1,k+2}$ falls below $\theta$."


From method.tex lines 36-40 (Eq. 3):
"$\Kempirical = \max\{k : \text{CI}_{\text{lower}}^{95\%}(\auc_{k,k+1}) > \theta\}$"

The introduction defines K* with two conditions - requiring both that the current pair's lower CI exceeds θ AND the next pair's upper CI falls below θ - while the methods section uses only the first condition. The introduction's definition is more restrictive, demanding evidence that the current pair is distinguishable while the next pair is not.

Looking more closely at what these actually mean: the methods definition finds the maximum k where the lower confidence bound of AUC_{k,k+1} exceeds θ, which implicitly means the lower bound of AUC_{k+1,k+2} drops to or below θ. But the introduction's requirement that the upper CI of AUC_{k+1,k+2} falls below θ is stricter - it ensures the entire confidence interval sits below θ, not just the lower bound. The two definitions are genuinely inconsistent—the introduction requires the upper confidence bound of the next pair to fall below θ, which is stricter than the method's formal definition that only requires the lower bound to fail. For K*=2, this distinction might not matter in practice if both bounds happen to be below threshold, but formally they're different and should be aligned. I should also flag the hardcoded section reference while I'm at it.
```

level=logging.INFO,\\n    format=\\\"%(asctime)s [%(levelname)s] %(message)s\\\",\\n    datefmt=\\\"%H:%M:%S\\\",\\n)\\nlog = logging.getLogger(__name__)\\n\\n\\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n# Utility: simple 4-gram BLEU (no nltk dependency)\\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n\\ndef ngrams(tokens, n):\\n    return [tuple(tokens[i : i + n]) for i in range(len(tokens) - n + 1)]\\n\\n\\ndef simple_bleu(candidate_tokens, reference_tokens, max_n=4):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Compute sentence-level BLEU up to max_n grams with brevity penalty.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    if len(candidate_tokens) == 0:\\n        return 0.0\\n    precisions = []\\n    for n in range(1, max_n + 1):\\n        cand_ngrams = ngrams(candidate_tokens, n)\\n        ref_ngrams = ngrams(reference_tokens, n)\\n        if not cand_ngrams:\\n            precisions.append(0.0)\\n            continue\\n        ref_counts = Counter(ref_ngrams)\\n        clipped = 0\\n        cand_counts = Counter(cand_ngrams)\\n        for ng, cnt in cand_counts.items():\\n            clipped += min(cnt, ref_counts.get(ng, 0))\\n        precisions.append(clipped / len(cand_ngrams))\\n    if any(p == 0 for p in precisions):\\n        return 0.0\\n    log_avg = sum(math.log(p) for p in precisions) / max_n\\n    bp = min(1.0, math.exp(1 - len(reference_tokens) / max(len(candidate_tokens), 1)))\\n    return bp * math.exp(log_avg)\\n\\n\\n# ===================================================================\\n# Step 2: Seed data preparation\\n# ===================================================================\\n\\ndef prepare_seed_data(tokenizer, data_dir: Path, num_samples: int, max_tokens: int = 256):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Download C4 validation, truncate to max_tokens, save as depth_0.jsonl.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    out_path = data_dir / \\\"depth_0.jsonl\\\"\\n    if out_path.exists() and sum(1 for _ in open(out_path)) >= num_samples:\\n        log.info(\\\"depth_0.jsonl already exists with enough samples — skipping\\\")\\n        return\\n\\n    from datasets import load_dataset\\n\\n    # Use a single validation shard to avoid slow full-tree listing on proxied networks\\n    hf_endpoint = os.environ.get(\\\"HF_ENDPOINT\\\", \\\"https://huggingface.co\\\")\\n    C4_SHARD_URL = f\\\"{hf_endpoint}/datasets/allenai/c4/resolve/main/en/c4-validation.00000-of-00008.json.gz\\\"\\n    log.info(\\\"Loading C4 validation shard (streaming) ...\\\")\\n    try:\\n        ds = load_dataset(\\\"json\\\", data_files=C4_SHARD_URL, split=\\\"train\\\", streaming=True)\\n    except Exception as e:\\n        log.warning(f\\\"Direct shard download failed ({e}), falling back to full dataset listing\\\")\\n        ds = load_dataset(\\\"allenai/c4\\\", \\\"en\\\", split=\\\"validation\\\", streaming=True)\\n\\n    records = []\\n    for i, example in enumerate(tqdm(ds, total=num_samples, desc=\\\"Sampling C4\\\")):\\n        if i >= num_samples:\\n            break\\n        text = example[\\\"text\\\"].strip()\\n        if not text:\\n            continue\\n        ids = tokenizer.encode(text, add_special_tokens=False)[:max_tokens]\\n        truncated = tokenizer.decode(ids, skip_special_tokens=True)\\n        records.append({\\\"text\\\": truncated, \\\"doc_id\\\": len(records)})\\n        if len(records) >= num_samples:\\n            break\\n\\n    data_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\\n    with open(out_path, \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        for r in records:\\n            f.write(json.dumps(r, ensure_ascii=False) + \\\"\\\\n\\\")\\n    log.info(f\\\"Saved {len(records)} seed documents to {out_path}\\\")\\n\\n... [截断: 输出过长，省略 13282 字符] ...\\n\\narning_rate=0.05,\\n                num_leaves=31, verbose=-1, n_jobs=-1,\\n            )\\n            clf.fit(X[train_idx], y[train_idx])\\n            proba = clf.predict_proba(X[test_idx])[:, 1]\\n            fold_aucs.append(roc_auc_score(y[test_idx], proba))\\n        auc_mean = float(np.mean(fold_aucs))\\n        auc_std = float(np.std(fold_aucs))\\n        pairwise_auc[f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"] = round(auc_mean, 4)\\n        pairwise_auc_folds[f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"] = [round(a, 4) for a in fold_aucs]\\n        log.info(f\\\"  AUC({k-1} vs {k}) = {auc_mean:.4f} ± {auc_std:.4f}  folds={[round(a,4) for a in fold_aucs]}\\\")\\n\\n    with open(results_dir / \\\"pairwise_auc.json\\\", \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        json.dump({\\\"mean\\\": pairwise_auc, \\\"per_fold\\\": pairwise_auc_folds}, f, indent=2)\\n\\n    # --- JSD ---\\n    log.info(\\\"Computing pairwise JSD ...\\\")\\n    pairwise_jsd = {}\\n    for k in range(1, max_depth + 1):\\n        mask_prev = depths == k - 1\\n        mask_curr = depths == k\\n        feat_prev = feat_matrix[mask_prev]\\n        feat_curr = feat_matrix[mask_curr]\\n\\n        jsds_per_feat = []\\n        for j in range(feat_matrix.shape[1]):\\n            a = feat_prev[:, j]\\n            b = feat_curr[:, j]\\n            # histogram-based JSD\\n            lo = min(a.min(), b.min())\\n            hi = max(a.max(), b.max())\\n            if hi - lo < 1e-12:\\n                jsds_per_feat.append(0.0)\\n                continue\\n            bins = np.linspace(lo, hi, 51)\\n            p_hist = np.histogram(a, bins=bins, density=True)[0] + 1e-12\\n            q_hist = np.histogram(b, bins=bins, density=True)[0] + 1e-12\\n            p_hist /= p_hist.sum()\\n            q_hist /= q_hist.sum()\\n            m = 0.5 * (p_hist + q_hist)\\n            jsd = 0.5 * np.sum(p_hist * np.log2(p_hist / m)) + 0.5 * np.sum(q_hist * np.log2(q_hist / m))\\n            jsds_per_feat.append(float(jsd))\\n\\n        jsd_mean = float(np.mean(jsds_per_feat))\\n        pairwise_jsd[f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"] = round(jsd_mean, 6)\\n        log.info(f\\\"  JSD({k-1} vs {k}) = {jsd_mean:.6f}\\\")\\n\\n    with open(results_dir / \\\"jsd.json\\\", \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        json.dump(pairwise_jsd, f, indent=2)\\n\\n    # --- K* ---\\n    k_star = 0\\n    for k in range(1, max_depth + 1):\\n        key = f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"\\n        if pairwise_auc.get(key, 0) > 0.60:\\n            k_star = k\\n\\n    # --- Ordinal classification ---\\n    log.info(\\\"Running ordinal LightGBM classification ...\\\")\\n    gkf_ord = GroupKFold(n_splits=5)\\n    all_preds = np.zeros_like(depths, dtype=float)\\n    all_true = depths.copy()\\n\\n    importances = np.zeros(len(ALL_FEAT_COLS))\\n    for train_idx, test_idx in gkf_ord.split(feat_matrix, depths, groups=doc_ids):\\n        clf = lgb.LGBMClassifier(\\n            n_estimators=300, max_depth=8, learning_rate=0.05,\\n            num_leaves=63, verbose=-1, n_jobs=-1,\\n            num_class=max_depth + 1,\\n            objective=\\\"multiclass\\\",\\n        )\\n        clf.fit(feat_matrix[train_idx], depths[train_idx])\\n        preds = clf.predict(feat_matrix[test_idx])\\n        all_preds[test_idx] = preds\\n        importances += clf.feature_importances_\\n\\n    importances /= 5.0\\n    mae = float(mean_absolute_error(all_true, all_preds))\\n    accuracy = float(accuracy_score(all_true, all_preds.astype(int)))\\n    log.info(f\\\"  Ordinal MAE = {mae:.4f}, Accuracy = {accuracy:.4f}, K* = {k_star}\\\")\\n\\n    ordinal_results = {\\\"mae\\\": round(mae, 4), \\\"accuracy\\\": round(accuracy, 4), \\\"k_star\\\": k_star}\\n    with open(results_dir / \\\"ordinal_results.json\\\", \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        json.dump(ordinal_results, f, indent=2)\\n\\n    # --- Feature importance ---\\n    feat_imp = sorted(\\n        [{\\\"feature\\\": ALL_FEAT_COLS[i], \\\"importance\\\": float(importances[i])} for i in range(len(ALL_FEAT_COLS))],\\n        key=lambda x: x[\\\"importance\\\"],\\n        reverse=True,\\n    )[:10]\\n    with open(results_dir / \\\"feature_importance.json\\\", \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        json.dump(feat_imp, f, indent=2)\\n\\n    # --- Summary ---\\n    summary_lines = [\\n        \\\"=\\\" * 60,\\n        \\\"MVP Pilot Saturation Study — Results Summary\\\",\\n        \\\"=\\\" * 60,\\n        \\\"\\\",\\n        \\\"Pairwise AUC (adjacent depths):\\\",\\n    ]\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 1.37}}"}

[截断: 输出过长，省略 13282 字符] ...\\n\\narning_rate=0.05,\\n                num_leaves=31, verbose=-1, n_jobs=-1,\\n            )\\n            clf.fit(X[train_idx], y[train_idx])\\n            proba = clf.predict_proba(X[test_idx])[:, 1]\\n            fold_aucs.append(roc_auc_score(y[test_idx], proba))\\n        auc_mean = float(np.mean(fold_aucs))\\n        auc_std = float(np.std(fold_aucs))\\n        pairwise_auc[f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"] = round(auc_mean, 4)\\n        pairwise_auc_folds[f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"] = [round(a, 4) for a in fold_aucs]\\n        log.info(f\\\"  AUC({k-1} vs {k}) = {auc_mean:.4f} ± {auc_std:.4f}  folds={[round(a,4) for a in fold_aucs]}\\\")\\n\\n    with open(results_dir / \\\"pairwise_auc.json\\\", \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        json.dump({\\\"mean\\\": pairwise_auc, \\\"per_fold\\\": pairwise_auc_folds}, f, indent=2)\\n\\n    # --- JSD ---\\n    log.info(\\\"Computing pairwise JSD ...\\\")\\n    pairwise_jsd = {}\\n    for k in range(1, max_depth + 1):\\n        mask_prev = depths == k - 1\\n        mask_curr = depths == k\\n        feat_prev = feat_matrix[mask_prev]\\n        feat_curr = feat_matrix[mask_curr]\\n\\n        jsds_per_feat = []\\n        for j in range(feat_matrix.shape[1]):\\n            a = feat_prev[:, j]\\n            b = feat_curr[:, j]\\n            # histogram-based JSD\\n            lo = min(a.min(), b.min())\\n            hi = max(a.max(), b.max())\\n            if hi - lo < 1e-12:\\n                jsds_per_feat.append(0.0)\\n                continue\\n            bins = np.linspace(lo, hi, 51)\\n            p_hist = np.histogram(a, bins=bins, density=True)[0] + 1e-12\\n            q_hist = np.histogram(b, bins=bins, density=True)[0] + 1e-12\\n            p_hist /= p_hist.sum()\\n            q_hist /= q_hist.sum()\\n            m = 0.5 * (p_hist + q_hist)\\n            jsd = 0.5 * np.sum(p_hist * np.log2(p_hist / m)) + 0.5 * np.sum(q_hist * np.log2(q_hist / m))\\n            jsds_per_feat.append(float(jsd))\\n\\n        jsd_mean = float(np.mean(jsds_per_feat))\\n        pairwise_jsd[f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"] = round(jsd_mean, 6)\\n        log.info(f\\\"  JSD({k-1} vs {k}) = {jsd_mean:.6f}\\\")\\n\\n    with open(results_dir / \\\"jsd.json\\\", \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        json.dump(pairwise_jsd, f, indent=2)\\n\\n    # --- K* ---\\n    k_star = 0\\n    for k in range(1, max_depth + 1):\\n        key = f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"\\n        if pairwise_auc.get(key, 0) > 0.60:\\n            k_star = k\\n\\n    # --- Ordinal classification ---\\n    log.info(\\\"Running ordinal LightGBM classification ...\\\")\\n    gkf_ord = GroupKFold(n_splits=5)\\n    all_preds = np.zeros_like(depths, dtype=float)\\n    all_true = depths.copy()\\n\\n    importances = np.zeros(len(ALL_FEAT_COLS))\\n    for train_idx, test_idx in gkf_ord.split(feat_matrix, depths, groups=doc_ids):\\n        clf = lgb.LGBMClassifier(\\n            n_estimators=300, max_depth=8, learning_rate=0.05,\\n            num_leaves=63, verbose=-1, n_jobs=-1,\\n            num_class=max_depth + 1,\\n            objective=\\\"multiclass\\\",\\n        )\\n        clf.fit(feat_matrix[train_idx], depths[train_idx])\\n        preds = clf.predict(feat_matrix[test_idx])\\n        all_preds[test_idx] = preds\\n        importances += clf.feature_importances_\\n\\n    importances /= 5.0\\n    mae = float(mean_absolute_error(all_true, all_preds))\\n    accuracy = float(accuracy_score(all_true, all_preds.astype(int)))\\n    log.info(f\\\"  Ordinal MAE = {mae:.4f}, Accuracy = {accuracy:.4f}, K* = {k_star}\\\")\\n\\n    ordinal_results = {\\\"mae\\\": round(mae, 4), \\\"accuracy\\\": round(accuracy, 4), \\\"k_star\\\": k_star}\\n    with open(results_dir / \\\"ordinal_results.json\\\", \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        json.dump(ordinal_results, f, indent=2)\\n\\n    # --- Feature importance ---\\n    feat_imp = sorted(\\n        [{\\\"feature\\\": ALL_FEAT_COLS[i], \\\"importance\\\": float(importances[i])} for i in range(len(ALL_FEAT_COLS))],\\n        key=lambda x: x[\\\"importance\\\"],\\n        reverse=True,\\n    )[:10]\\n    with open(results_dir / \\\"feature_importance.json\\\", \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        json.dump(feat_imp, f, indent=2)\\n\\n    # --- Summary ---\\n    summary_lines = [\\n        \\\"=\\\" * 60,\\n        \\\"MVP Pilot Saturation Study — Results Summary\\\",\\n        \\\"=\\\" * 60,\\n        \\\"\\\",\\n        \\\"Pairwise AUC (adjacent depths):\\\",\\n    ]\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 1.37}}"

_source line 1841 · 2026-05-13T19:34:42.651Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Found it. The key line is from `run_exp010_ordinal_comparison.py`:
> "1. Ordinal Binary Decomposition (OBD) with LightGBM — K-1 binary classifiers for P(Y>k)"

And `exp006_filtering_strategies.py`:
> "OBD-LightGBM: K-1 binary classifiers for P(depth>k), then exp(-alpha * E[depth])."

So the code uses P(Y > k), i.e., P(depth > k), NOT P(depth ≤ k). 


But the paper specifies P(depth ≤ k), which is the complement. The two formulations are mathematically equivalent — finding the minimum k where P(depth > k) < 0.5 gives the same result as finding where P(depth ≤ k) > 0.5 — so the final predictions should be identical despite the notational difference.

I need to check the actual implementation in exp010 to see which direction they're using and make sure my code matches their approach.
```

</details>

in rows])\\n    doc_ids = np.array([int(r[\\\"doc_id\\\"]) for r in rows])\\n    X = np.array([\\n        [float(r[c]) if r[c] not in (\\\"\\\", \\\"nan\\\") else np.nan for c in FEAT_COLS]\\n        for r in rows\\n    ])\\n    col_means = np.nanmean(X, axis=0)\\n    for j in range(X.shape[1]):\\n        mask = np.isnan(X[:, j])\\n        X[mask, j] = col_means[j]\\n    return X, depths, doc_ids\\n\\n\\ndef pairwise_auc(y_true, y_pred_proba_matrix, max_depth):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Compute AUC for each adjacent depth pair using predicted class probabilities.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    results = {}\\n    for k in range(1, max_depth + 1):\\n        mask = (y_true == k - 1) | (y_true == k)\\n        if mask.sum() < 10:\\n            results[f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"] = float(\\\"nan\\\")\\n            continue\\n        y_bin = (y_true[mask] == k).astype(int)\\n        if len(np.unique(y_bin)) < 2:\\n            results[f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"] = float(\\\"nan\\\")\\n            continue\\n        # Use the probability of the higher class\\n        if y_pred_proba_matrix is not None and y_pred_proba_matrix.ndim == 2:\\n            score = y_pred_proba_matrix[mask, k]\\n        else:\\n round(float(roc_auc_score(y_bin, score)), 4)\\n        except ValueError:\\n            results[f\\\"{k-1}v{k}\\\"] = float(\\\"nan\\\")\\n    return results\\n\\n\\ndef method_obd_lgbm(X, y, groups, max_depth, n_splits):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Ordinal Binary Decomposition: K-1 binary LGBMs for P(Y > k).\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    n = len(y)\\n    # Store cumulative probabilities P(Y > k) for k=0..K-2\\n    cum_probs = np.zeros((n, max_depth))  # cum_probs[:, k] = P(Y > k)\\n\\n    gkf = GroupKFold(n_splits=n_splits)\\n    fold_indices = list(gkf.split(X, y, groups=groups))\\n\\n    for k in range(max_depth):  # k = 0, 1, ..., K-2 (K-1 classifiers)\\n        y_bin = (y > k).astype(int)\\n        for train_idx, test_idx in fold_indices:\\n            clf = lgb.LGBMClassifier(\\n cum_probs[test_idx, k] = clf.predict_proba(X[test_idx])[:, 1]\\n\\n    # Convert cumulative probs to class probs\\n    # P(Y=0) = 1 - P(Y>0)\\n    # P(Y=k) = P(Y>k-1) - P(Y>k) for 0 < k < K\\n    # P(Y=K) = P(Y>K-1)\\n    n_classes = max_depth + 1\\n    class_probs = np.zeros((n, n_classes))\\n    class_probs[:, 0] = 1.0 - cum_probs[:, 0]\\n    for k in range(1, max_depth):\\n        class_probs[:, k] = cum_probs[:, k - 1] - cum_probs[:, k]\\n    class_probs[:, max_depth] = cum_probs[:, max_depth - 1]\\n    # Clip negatives from estimation noise\\n    class_probs = <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can see the complete OBD implementation:

**Code (lines 89-124):**
- Binary classifiers estimate `P(Y > k)` for k = 0, 1, ..., K-2 (line 100: `y_bin = (y > k).astype(int)`)
- Cumulative probabilities stored as `cum_probs[:, k] = P(Y > k)` (line 93)
- Converted to class probabilities: `P(Y=0) = 1 - P(Y>0)`, `P(Y=k) = P(Y>k-1) - P(Y>k)`, `P(Y=K) = P(Y>K-1)`


- Final prediction takes the argmax of these class probabilities to select the most likely class.

**Paper (method.tex lines 96-103):**
- Sub-classifier estimates `P(depth ≤ k | x)`, k = 0, 1, ..., K-1
- Predicted depth: `min{k : P(depth ≤ k | x) > 0.5}`

Key differences:
1. **Direction**: Code uses P(Y > k), paper uses P(depth ≤ k). These are complements: `P(depth ≤ k) = 1 - P(Y > k)`.
2. **Prediction method**: Code uses `argmax` on derived class probabilities, while the paper uses `min{k : P(depth ≤ k) > 0.5}`. These aren't equivalent in general—`argmax` picks the most probable class, whereas the threshold approach finds the median of the distribution. Let me work through whether they're actually equivalent here. Converting the paper's formulation: `min{k : P(depth ≤ k) > 0.5} = min{k : 1 - P(Y > k) > 0.5} = min{k : P(Y > k) < 0.5}`, which is finding the first k where the cumulative probability crosses 0.5.

So the code's mode and the paper's median can diverge. Testing with [0.3, 0.4, 0.2, 0.1], both give 1, but with [0.25, 0.2, 0.3, 0.25], the mode is 2 and the median is also 2. Let me check a case where they actually differ.

With [0.1, 0.4, 0.1, 0.4], the argmax picks index 1 as the mode, but the cumulative distribution crosses 0.5 at index 2, making the median 2. So they can disagree.

The real issue is that the code uses argmax for prediction while the paper uses the median—that's a genuine algorithmic difference, not just a notational one. The direction convention about how probabilities are computed is just cosmetic. I should check what the worker actually found in their audit, since this discrepancy seems like exactly what they were supposed to catch.

Now I need to see what the Agent concluded after the worker finished. Let me look at whether method.tex was updated to reflect any corrections.
```
